Finance (IHLEG)
$1.3 trillion a year by 2035
COP31 focus: first implementation update on the Baku-Belem
roadmap to $1.3T. This total is inclusive of the $300bn NCQG target.
Context: COP29 set the New Collective Quantified Goal (NCQG),
calling on all actors to scale climate finance for developing countries
to at least $1.3tn a year by 2035. No country has an assigned share,

and private capital is expected to count.
At COP31: first implementation update on the Baku-Belem roadmap
to $1.3T, based on “measurable progress” against targets including:
• a global monitoring framework to track progress in climate finance
mobilization, identify bottlenecks, and highlight emerging solutions.
• clear pathways for public, private, domestic and international
finance;
• Expanding operability, comparability, and interoperability of

climate-finance data; and
• action on grants and concessional finance, debt and fiscal space,
private-finance costs, capacity and system reform.
Are the NCQG and IHLEG aligned? IHLEG’s $1.3tn external-finance
estimate (within the $3.2tn needed excl. China) does align with the
NCQG $1.3tn goal and underpins the Baku-to-Belém Roadmap.
However, they are built differently: political target vs needs-based
estimate.
$300 billion a year by 2035
COP31 focus: what counts and who contributes. This amount

contributes towards the $1.3tn NCQG target from all actors.
Context: Developed countries agreed to lead mobilisation of at least
$300bn a year by 2035, counting public finance and the private
finance it mobilises; no country has a fixed share.
At COP31: Focus turns to how to deliver. Developing countries are
pushing for the development of action plans to chart a pathway to
$300bn and for the Article 9 work programme to be included on the
formal agenda (with emphasis on Article 9.1 – which explicitly covers

developed country finance provision).
Adaptation finance
COP31 focus: embedding and tracking the tripling pledge
Context: COP30 called for adaptation finance to at least triple by
2035, yet this was relatively vague; it did not specify a base year or a
target figure (some have suggested $120bn, i.e. 3x the 2025 goal).
At COP31: Parties need to resolve disagreement on whether to
anchor the pledge in the Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) and put a
finer point on the agreed Belém Adaptation Indicators that will track

it, after disagreement stalled the June Bonn talks.
Loss and Damage Fund
COP31 focus: guidance, replenishment and access
Why it matters: The Fund is the only dedicated channel for
irreversible climate impacts, yet it has no annual funding target and
resources remain far below demand.
At COP31: Focus will be on its long-term funding strategy, with the
first replenishment cycle set to begin in 2027. $342m made available
in the first funding window, which offers $5–20m grants with at least

half reserved for SIDS and LDCs; but the voluntary nature of
payments, disputes over who pays and sensitivity around
“compensation” make major new pledges in Antalya unlikely.

Hurdles to these
targets
Grants vs loans

Developing countries want grants (especially fo r
adaptation), citing debt burdens and historical
responsibility; funders favour loans and blended
structures that include private capital.
Pressure on development banks
The US is pushing MDBs (e.g. the World Bank), to drop
their climate focus, according to the World Resources
Institute (WRI). Developing countries want MDB reform
to deliver more investment and faster disbursement.
Competing budget pressures

Donors are diverting aid to fund domestic priorities
such as defence. There is also the reality that climate is
now a lower priority for some governments.
Reliance on private capital
The IHLEG estimates ~½ the $1.3tn could come from
private sources. Private capital is looking for regulation,
insurance and more guarantees to reduce risk.

### Page 8

TO P I C  3 | AD AP TATI O N  &  R ES I L I EN C E (1  o f 2 )
Adaptation will be one of the most important, and more divisive, negotiating topics
Adaptation is also set to be on the formal negotiating agenda at COP31. UN warnings that an overshoot of the Paris Agreement’s 1.5°C goal is now unavoidable (at least temporarily), have
dampened morale for climate activists. As it relates to the COP agenda on adaptation, parties appear deadlocked on whether the COP30 goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035 should be

included in the Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) text. This is important, as anchoring it to the GGA could signal to investors the scale and timing of future public and blended finance flows.
The role of adaptation in the COP process
The Global Goal on Adaptation (GGA) was established under the Paris Agreement to enhance
adaptive capacity, strengthen resilience and reduce vulnerability to climate change. At COP30
last year, countries adopted 59 indicators (still being refined) to measure progress against the

goal. They also agreed a goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035. The pledge was vague: it set
neither a concrete figure nor a baseline year (a potential annual target of $120bn would be
triple the previous goal of c.$40bn by 2025).
Why GGA talks were deadlocked at the June climate meetings
Finance was the main sticking point at the June Bonn meetings. Developing countries wanted
the COP30 goal to triple adaptation finance by 2035 referenced in the GGA text; countries

including Canada, Norway and Japan opposed. Parties also split on whether the indicators task
force should be expert- or party-led, so no GGA text was agreed and the decision passes to
COP31. Moving the Adaptation Fund to serve the Paris Agreement exclusively (funded by a 5%
share of proceeds from Article 6.4 credits) stalled in Bonn over the composition of its board;
talks will resume at COP31.
What is needed at COP31 to unlock adaptation progress

Adaptation is on the provisional COP31 agenda, including the tripling adaptation finance goal
(though this may get blocked). Some parties want it explicitly referenced in the final COP31
GGA decision; this should become clearer when the agenda is adopted as COP31 opens on 9
Nov. At issue: the tripling pledge still lacks a baseline and named contributors. At COP30,
developed nations resisted new finance obligations, while the EU and many others pushed for

a roadmap to transition away from fossil fuels, which a group of mostly oil- and gas-producing
countries blocked. The result was a looser tripling goal and no agreed fossil-fuel roadmap.
Developing countries are unlikely to give ground, as adaptation relies far more on public
funding than mitigation, so a repeat of COP30’s finance vs. mitigation debate is plausible.
Sources: United Nations, UNEP, Carbon Brief, European Parliament (COP30 outcomes briefing, December 2025)

### Page 9

TO P I C  3 | AD AP TATI O N  &  R ES I L I EN C E (2  o f 2 )
The EU’s proposed Climate Resilience Law plans for a 3°C world, while reiterating commitment to net zero 2050
The EU Commission’s recently proposed Climate Resilience Law would set a reference global warming trajectory of 3°C, providing a benchmark for member states’ adaptation planning. The EU
states it remains committed to reaching net zero by 2050, but some at COP will likely view the proposal as accepting overshoot. With the EU also pointing to an estimated need for EUR70bn in

annual adaptation investment, this provides an interesting backdrop as COP31 seeks to resume adaptation negotiations which stalled earlier this year, primarily due to finance.
What the Commission proposed
On 27 October the Commission presented its EU Framework for Climate Resilience, anchored
by a proposed European Climate Resilience Law. With current global policy commitments
pointing to around 2°C of warming by 2050 and 3°C by 2100, the law would set a common

reference trajectory of 1.5°C in 2030, 2°C in 2050 and 3°C in 2100, with this regarded as the
“baseline assumption”. Member states would be required to use it in national climate risk
assessments and adopt a single national resilience plan by end-2030.
Why it matters: the EU is planning for overshoot
The EU highlighted warnings that overshoot of the 1.5°C goal is now “unavoidable” (even if just
temporarily) as necessitating policy action, even as the EU says it is still committed to climate

neutrality by 2050. The Commission links the plan to Europe’s hottest summer on record and
estimates annual economic losses from climate extremes of €50bn over the past four years,
adding that output losses caused by climate change go far beyond this. It says EU adaptation
needs are ~€70bn a year through to 2050, with Europe warming at 2x the global rate.
What it means for COP31
The proposal demonstrates that warnings on the implications of a significant overshoot of the

Paris Agreement global warming limits are moving from scientific theory to real government
policy. While the opening line of the new EU framework emphasized it remains committed to
reaching net zero by 2050, some may view a 3°C planning baseline as accepting overshoot.
The headline €70bn annual EU adaptation finance requirement also provides an interesting
backdrop heading into Global Goal on Adaptation talks given they are stalled largely due to

disagreement over explicit links to finance in the GGA text. For context, the UNEP estimates
annual adaptation needs for developing countries of $310–365bn by 2035. Ultimately, we
expect adaptation focus to continue growing as warming impacts are increasingly felt and
societal calls for relief grow louder. Corporates will need to consider potential strategic
impacts, while some investors have already begun raising their baseline warming forecasts.

### Page 10

TO P I C  4 | EL EC TR I F I C ATI O N
Türkiye is championing a voluntary pledge to lift electrification from 23% to 35% by 2035
Electrification is expected to be at the centre of this “implementation COP”, targeting a rise in electricity’s share of global final energy use from 23% to 35% by 2035. We expect support for this
action given it is being positioned alongside energy security, but we also expect discussion and debate over how it is delivered (e.g., the role of renewables, nuclear and natural gas), alongside

concerns over clean tech dependency in supply chains. China and the EU have expressed support but not yet committed.
Some may brand this the electrification COP
The Action Agenda pledge aims to raise electricity's share of global final energy from 23%
today to 35% by 2035. The pledge was launched in Bonn this past June and formalised as the
"35-by-35 Global Electrification Pledge" in New York on 21 September.
China and the EU have signaled support, but have not yet signed the pledge

Electrification fits modern day energy-security goals making it a viable target for this year’s
COP. China’s cleantech dominance means it has a further incentive to support initiatives that
seek to increase the pace of global electrification. It also aligns closely with China's existing
development trajectory. While the 35% goal is based on Paris-aligned pathways supported by
IEA and IRENA analysis, the numerical target itself does not require the additional electricity to

come from non fossil fuel sources. This has invited some criticism, though it may make it
easier for some nations to sign the non-binding pledge.
Alignment with the voluntary renewables pledge
Recall that COP28 called for tripling global renewable power capacity by 2030 in COP28’s
formal Global Stocktake decision, equivalent to around 11.2 TW. IRENA’s September 2026
assessment puts capacity at 5.15 TW at end-2025. Record additions of 693 GW in 2025 remain

below the 1,200 GW needed annually in 2026–30. The 35-by-35 pledge complements this
supply goal by shifting transport, buildings and industry towards electricity, enabling
renewable power to replace fossil fuels. Its emissions benefit still depends on cleaner
generation.
We view the goal positively, but delivery needs grid enhancements.
IRENA counts ~2,500 GW of renewable, storage and large-load projects awaiting connection
and says annual investment to strengthen grid infrastructure and enhance system flexibility

must rise from ~$500bn in 2025 to around $1tn through 2035, and $1.2tn thereafter. That
said, signatories signal support, but domestic delivery plans will determine investment.
Sources: UNFCCC, COP31 Presidency, IEA, IRENA

TO P I C  5   |   F O S S I L  F U EL S
The fossil fuel transition roadmap track is growing outside the formal COP process
COP30 failed to agree a fossil fuel transition roadmap, despite support from more than 80 countries. Brazil’s COP30 presidency is therefore developing a roadmap outside formal UN
negotiations to be presented ahead of COP31. Separately, Colombia and the Netherlands convened a ‘coalition of the willing’ of 57 countries in Santa Marta in April 2026 to explore practical

steps towards a fossil fuel phase-out. This parallel forum allows willing countries to cooperate without needing consensus from all COP parties.
Sources: Carbon Brief, Santa Marta conference report

TO P I C  6 | TR AD E
Trade could resurface at COP31 as a challenge to collective agreement; talks have been requested
Trade tensions could feature at COP31 as governments weigh climate ambition against industrial competitiveness. Developing countries fear that measures such as the CBAM disadvantage

their exporters, while the EU says they prevent carbon leakage. COP30 agreed three annual dialogues on trade's role in climate cooperation (no binding outcome), creating for the first time a
formal space for trade discussions in the UNFCCC process, but The African Group and others would like it on the formal COP31 agenda.
Do trade discussions belong at COP?
Developing countries have long pushed back against climate-related trade measures such as
the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM) and Deforestation Regulation (EUDR).

They argue such measures are protectionism disguised as climate policy and
disproportionately impact poorer countries. Developed countries view such measures as
necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases where foreign competitors face
lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic industries, while also arguing
that COP is not the appropriate forum to discuss trade matters. Despite this, a European
Parliament briefing noted trade is a point of “exposure” for the EU at COP31.

COP30’s agreement on trade dialogue was notable, but it may not be enough
COP30 agreed three annual climate-trade dialogues at the mid-year sessions in 2026-28, with
a high-level event in 2028. The first, in Bonn (13 June), was calmer than expected but exposed
the divide: developing countries argued that climate-linked trade measures such as CBAM
raise costs and restrict market access and called for impact assessments within the UNFCCC,
while the EU defended them as responses to environmental externalities. With trade also

raised in other Bonn rooms, it is far from clear the dialogue will contain the issue in Antalya.
The African Group of negotiators has requested a Trade dialogue on the formal agenda.
China has already said it intends to use COP31 to oppose cleantech trade barriers, with its vice
minister of the Ministry of Ecology and Environment saying the event should discuss “in depth”
the “new problem of unilateralism and protectionism undermining global collective efforts” on

climate. The African Group is also pushing for trade to be on the formal agenda. Ultimately,
trade talks could prove more consequential at COP31 than in previous years given inflationary
pressures and concerns over business competitiveness, but it is hard to assess the potential
impact other than possibly causing delays in agreement. However, even if the impact is
limited, the issue could shape negotiations elsewhere outside of the COP process.
Sources: European Parliament, Carbon Brief, Reuters

### Page 13

TOP I C 7 | G EO P O L I TI CS  & MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (1  o f 2 )
Multilateralism is at a crossroads
Geopolitical fragmentation and the US exit from the Paris Agreement make consensus among nearly 200 nations harder, encouraging “coalitions of the willing” (outside of the formal COP
negotiations) to pursue more ambitious climate goals. COP31’s success or failure in securing multilateral agreement or credible voluntary pledges could shape future COPs by showing which

approaches deliver progress. The US retreat from the Paris Agreement will also continue to have far-reaching implications for climate finance. Overall, the EU and China will be expected to lead.
The consensus agreement principle under strain
The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) provides the
multilateral framework for COP, the forum where countries negotiate collective climate
decisions. The process is anchored by the view that all countries must act on climate change,

but the extent of their efforts depends on national circumstances. This puts the onus on
developed countries to lead. The question is whether this principle still holds against a more
fragmented backdrop and transactional diplomatic context as national budgets prioritize
security and business competitiveness. With the COP process still separating developed and
developing countries by their 1992 UNFCCC classifications, developed countries are continuing

to push for wealthier developing nations such as China and Saudi Arabia to play a greater role
on finance. This has been a major point of contention at recent summits.
The US stepping back has far-reaching implications
The US skipped COP30 and left the Paris Agreement. It will formally exit the UNFCCC in
February 2027. This retreat has weighed on broader climate-finance decisions, both from a
monetary sense and through the loss of negotiating power. While cities, states and businesses

from the US continue to support COP efforts (e.g., groups like America Is All In), the federal
administration’s absence has put more pressure on the EU and China to lead.
What does an evolving process look like?
Because formal COP decisions require consensus (or lack of objection), progress often stalls or
moves slowly. COP30 saw Brazil move items where there was strong, but not unanimous,
support such as the fossil fuel roadmap, to separate streams outside the formal COP process.

Countries disappointed with the lack of stronger mitigation action also launched a separate
summit on transitioning away from fossil fuels, held this past April in Colombia. While COP is
the flagship global forum, these actions demonstrate a shift towards “coalitions of the willing”
for issues where global agreement is not possible.
Sources: EU Council, EU Parliament, Carbon Brief, UN, WRI

TO P I C  7 | G EO P O L I TI C S  &  MU L TI L ATER AL I S M (2  o f 2 )
Much will rest on the approach of the EU and China

With the US absent, the EU and China are the two actors with the greatest diplomatic and economic leverage with which to broker compromise agreements between the different factions at
COP31. This will require careful diplomacy, with their interests not fully aligned in some areas; discussions around trade and climate finance may be particularly challenging. At the same time,
both have reiterated their continued support of climate action and multilateral decision making; a successful COP outcome would back up these commitments.

### Page 15

TO P I C  8 | AI  &  C L I MATE
The Antalya AI Pledge puts data-centre power demand on the agenda
We expect AI’s climate potential and rising electricity demand to make it a prominent COP31 topic, prompting debate over COPs’ role in data-centre energy use. The Antalya AI Pledge invites
governments and companies to commit voluntarily to sustainable infrastructure and environmental transparency. Its call for zero- and low-emissions energy infrastructure leaves room for

nuclear power, prompting discussion of its role in meeting data-centre demand alongside renewables.
The Antalya AI Pledge seeks to build stakeholder support for sustainable AI buildout
Türkiye is encouraging stakeholders (inc. corporates) to commit to the pledge, asking
signatories to “declare our shared ambition to make AI part of the climate solution. This
means sustainably powered, efficiently designed, transparently measured and available to all”.

Signatories will commit to: 1) Power and build AI infrastructure and hardware sustainably, 2)
Design, manage or procure AI responsibly across its lifecycle, 3) Support assessment and
reporting of the environmental impacts of AI systems, 4) Strengthen financial support and
technical infrastructure for AI for climate action.
The AI for Clean Technologies Initiative seeks to highlight AI climate solutions
The initiative plans to develop AI-enabled pilot projects (e.g. smart energy systems) and then

link the projects with industry partners and investors, aiming to demonstrate AI’s potential to
contribute to sustainability goals. For grids, the IEA sees AI improving forecasting, congestion
management and outage response, helping operators get more from existing lines.
The dialogue here will set the stage for AI discussions at future COPs
While the pledge is voluntary, many of these themes are already beginning to appear in early-
stage regulatory demands, with transparency and disclosure receiving particular focus. There

will be questions over how effective the pledge can be given the US has disengaged from the
COP process, though California could send a strong signal if it commits to the pledge. We will
be particularly focused on how the tech industry responds. Despite the trend of greenhushing,
we expect many to send delegations to Antalya, with AI companies and investors viewing
responsible practices and community engagement as key to avoiding backlash and delays to

AI buildout. Note that responsible AI was also a hot topic at the Principles for Responsible
Investment conference this year in October.
Sources: COP31 Presidency, IEA

### Page 16

TO P I C  9 | N ATU R E &  C AR B O N  MAR K ETS
Nature and carbon markets have key roles to play in supporting climate action
Carbon credit discussions will centre on government policies to boost demand and corporate engagement, with much of the formal UN architecture now in place. If carbon markets can scale,
the benefits would be wide-ranging (e.g. supporting emissions targets and providing finance for developing countries). Credibility and greenwashing concerns remain key risks however; we

expect prices to diverge in favour of high-integrity credits. The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF) now has ~$7.8bn of sponsor capital against a $10bn end-2026 target.
Sources: Tropical Forest Forever Facility, Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
16
Voluntary carbon market development
Türkiye has stated a focus on carbon markets at COP31, particularly on government actions to
boost demand, highlighting the potential for carbon markets to unlock more than $50bn in

additional annual finance for developing nations. The Coalition to Grow Carbon Markets
(made up of 13 governments) has announced it will unveil a “Policy Playbook” at COP31 to
support market development. While much of Article 6 (carbon credits under UN process) has
moved to delivery, countries will aim to reach agreement on the funding model for Article 6.2
at COP31. Further carbon market progress stands to have multiple benefits; it can support
developed countries in hitting climate goals while reducing immediate decarbonisation

requirements, while also providing finance and supporting ecosystems in developing ones.
Understanding the pushback
Carbon markets have seen pushback over credit integrity (including regulatory scrutiny) and
the risk that offsets delay domestic cuts. UN involvement is needed to enforce common
accounting and integrity rules, such as the Article 6 rulebook finalised at COP29 and due for
review in 2028, that stop the same reduction being claimed twice. Without that backstop,

fragmented bilateral and voluntary schemes risk eroding the trust needed to scale the market.
Update on the TFFF
The Tropical Forest Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30 last year, aims for $10bn of
sponsor contributions by year-end. It pays tropical-forest countries per hectare conserved,
discounts payments for each hectare lost or degraded, and directs at least 20% to Indigenous
Peoples and local communities. Its Charter was adopted on 22 July 2026 and received a

tailwind following the re-election of Brazilian President Lula, who is a key supporter. Shortly
after Lula’s re-election, the Netherlands pledged up to $500mn to the fund, bringing the total
pledged to $7.8bn. Further financing commitments are key, with Norway’s $3bn contribution
conditional on the fund reaching at least $10bn by the end of 2026.

TO P I C  1 0 | P AC I F I C , O C EANS  & S EA L EV EL  R I S E
Oceans will be a priority item, with Australia promising to champion Pacific interests
Australia’s ask for ceding the hosting was a Pacific pre-COP, and it has promised to continue championing Pacific interests at COP31. The Taku Pakasoa Declaration, signed at the pre-COP,

reflects Pacific priorities, calling for faster emissions cuts, more adaptation finance and financial system reform. The climate-ocean nexus will also be a focus, including investable solutions.
Championing Pacific interests is a key focus for Australia
As part of the hosting compromise, Australia co-hosted the official pre-COP with Fiji and
Tuvalu (5–8 Oct), attended by countries including Germany, the UAE and India (though some
questioned the low number of international leaders who attended). Australia has pledged to

keep championing Pacific interests as President of the Negotiations.
Taku Pakasoa Declaration reflects Pacific Priorities
Signed on 6 Oct at the pre-COP, it calls for faster emissions cuts, a substantial increase in
climate finance (particularly for adaptation in capacity-constrained countries), reform of the
international financial architecture, and for elevating the ocean-climate nexus. It also
welcomes the 35% electrification goal.
The challenges of rising sea levels are gaining increasing prominence

Sea levels in the region are rising faster than the global average, and half of Pacific Island
infrastructure lies within 500 metres of the coast. The UN General Assembly adopted the UN
Declaration on Sea Level Rise by consensus on 24 Sept; global sea levels rose nearly 6mm in
2024, the highest annual increase on record.
Oceans are key to climate stability, but more finance is needed
Pacific nations emphasise the critical role of oceans as a natural environmental regulator.

They absorb 30% of all carbon dioxide emissions (largest carbon sink) and capture 90% of the
excess heat generated by these emissions. Investor interest in sustainable ocean solutions is
growing, with 2026 YTD blue bond issuance already an annual record, yet bond issuance
supporting UN SDG 14: Life Below Water trails many other categories; scaling this up will be a
focus at COP31. A commitment to delivering “tangible, bankable ocean outcomes” at COP31 is

one of 13 points included as part of the Trabzon Ocean Call to Action unveiled by Türkiye,
while the ORRAA has unveiled a Blue Climate Finance Pathway.
Sources: Taku Pakasoa Declaration (UNFCCC), UN Declaration on Sea Level Rise

Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
Sources: UNFCCC; COP30 and COP31 Presidency documents; and the other sources referenced in this report
19
Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Preview  |  October 2026
TERM DEFINITION

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework for international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of carbon credits between countries.
Article 9

Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies (e.g. SB64) in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.
Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.

Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.
COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum is COP President and leads the Action Agenda.
TERM DEFINITION
Final COP text Decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome.

Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.
Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the link to finance is contested.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the

shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.
Negotiating groups Blocs that coordinate national positions, such as the G77 & China, the EU, LDCs and AOSIS.

Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
Pre-COP Ministerial meeting ahead of the COP to test positions and build consensus. For COP31, Australia co-hosted a Pacific pre-COP.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafts final texts and steers government-to-government talks.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

Appendix C: The level of alarm over global warming has begun to move higher (again)
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

## Document 397: India_ A middle class too small to supplant China’s economic role (2026-10-26)

Source PDF: all report pdf/India_ A middle class too small to supplant China’s economic role (2026-10-26).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
At a time when Chinese consumption is slowing significantly, could India become a driver of global consumption? For now, this
prospect presents considerable difficulties, with many obstacles to overcome. Despite dynamic growth and the Indian economy’s
expanding share of global output (8.2% of worldwide GDP in 2025), the middle-class consumer population remains modest and is nowhere
near the size of China’s.
This is due to the concentration of employment in low-value-added and largely informal sectors. The few manufacturing sectors with

high value-added and strong job potential — electrical equipment, electronics, automotive, chemicals and pharmaceuticals — continue to
face constraints from an unfavourable business environment and a skills shortage that is holding back their move into premium market
segments.
Furthermore, the highest-paying service sector jobs, particularly in information technology, are now threatened by the rise of
artificial intelligence. Without concrete measures, the “demographic window” — which is expected to remain favourable for another 15 to

25 years — could close before India establishes a solid industrial base and a sufficiently large middle class.
INDIA: A MIDDLE CLASS TOO SMALL TO SUPPLANT CHINA’S ECONOMIC ROLE
Johanna Melka
issue 20
26 October 2026

### Page 2

2
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoInsight 20 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
worldINDIA: A MIDDLE CLASS TOO SMALL TO SUPPLANT CHINA’S ECONOMIC ROLE
INDIA IS STRUGGLING TO ESTABLISH A ROBUST MIDDLE
CLASS
With an annual growth rate of 7.3% over the past decade (excluding the
pandemic), India has one of the highest growth rates among the world’s
major economies. Yet, its middle-class consumer base remains surpri-
singly small. When measured in current U.S. dollars, total consumption

is very high in India (USD 2,241 billion in 2025), but this figure prima -
rily reflects the country’s overall population size. By comparison, this
consumption level falls 3.5 times short of China’s corresponding figure.
Furthermore, per capita consumption adjusted for purchasing power
parity (PPP) is 1.3 times lower than that of Indonesia, the Philippines
and Vietnam. When evaluated at constant prices, consumption per ca-
pita has increased only slightly over the past thirty years compared to

China and even Vietnam (Chart 1).
According to our estimates, based on the research conducted by Ló -
pez-Calva and Ortiz-Juarez (2014) 1, India’s middle-class consumer
segment accounts for just 1.6% of the total population (see Table 1),
or fewer than 24 million people. This is less than in Thailand and Viet-
nam (more than 30 million each) and far below China (approximately
490 million, according to the same definition).
The majority of households identified as middle class by standard In -

dian research institutions (the Centre for Monitoring Indian Economy
[CMIE] and the National Council of Applied Economic Research [NCAER])
would, in fact, be categorised as “vulnerable” according to World Bank
criteria: households that have officially emerged from poverty yet lack
the financial stability necessary for sustained economic security.
Highly concentrated consumption and limited
upward mobility
The structure of Indian household consumption reveals significant

disparities between urban and rural areas. In 2024, average month -
ly consumption per capita amounted to 6,996 rupees in urban areas
1 These authors define the middle class as individuals for whom the risk of falling back into poverty is limited (less than 4% over four years), corresponding to daily per capita income
ranging from 17 to 98 2021 PPP dollars (with vulnerability thresholds at 6.85 and 14 2017 PPP dollars, according to the World Bank). In the absence of reliable national income

data collection mechanisms in India, our study applies these thresholds to the World Bank’s consumer expenditure data, an approach aligned with the objective of assessing actual
purchasing capacity deployed in the market (although this tends to slightly underestimate the middle class in terms of income, as household savings remain unaccounted for in
expenditure measurements).
(approximately USD 84.5), compared with 4,122 rupees in rural areas
(less than USD 50). The wealthiest 5% of urban consumers spent an

average equivalent of USD 245 per month per person. Consequently,
discretionary spending power remained concentrated among the hi -
ghest income brackets.
The structure of consumption has changed over the past decade. The
proportion allocated to food purchases (34.8% of total spending on ave-
rage) declined by 4.3 percentage points between 2011–12 and 2023–
24, while allocations to transportation and communications increased
to 12.4% (+3.8 pp). Although the allocation to durable goods has in -

creased, it remains below 7% of total household spending. This increase
was driven more by a rise in the use of credit than by income growth.
Over the past ten years, consumer loans have been the main source of
household debt. Although still modest relative to GDP , household debt
rose by 12.2 pp to reach 47.8% of GDP by the end of 2025, according to
the Bank for International Settlements (BIS).

THE LABOUR MARKET SUFFERS FROM STRUCTURAL
WEAKNESSES
The limited size of the middle class and the low level of household
consumption in India can be attributed, in particular, to the enduring
structural weaknesses of the labour market. The labour market faces
two major problems: insufficient job creation and the fact that most of
the jobs created are low-skilled and low-paid.

Insufficient job creation
Despite the Indian economy’s robust growth, its employment rate re -
mains substantially lower than in comparable Asian economies: 52.7%
in 2025 according to the International Labour Organization (ILO), com-
pared with 65.6% in Indonesia and 71.3% in Vietnam. This disparity
is mainly due to low labour force participation among women (34%
compared with 53.4% and 68%) and young people (40.8% of those aged
20-24 compared with 66.7% and 69.4%).

Over the past ten years, employment growth has averaged just 2.4%
annually, whereas GDP growth was close to 6% per year.
The services sector (representing 33.2% of employment in 2025) ac -
counted for the bulk of job creation during this period (+3.2% per year),
far ahead of the manufacturing sector (+1.4%) and, above all, agricultu-
re (+0.1%), which nevertheless remains the country’s largest employer
(41.5% of the workforce). This is not enough to absorb the 10 to 12 mil-

lion new entrants to the labour market each year.
2 Employment elasticity remained relatively stable between 1990 and 2012.
A limited number of sectors have significant po-
tential for creating high-quality, well-paid jobs
To identify sectors with high potential for job creation, we calculate
employment elasticity both for aggregate economy (KLEMS data) and
for the formal manufacturing sector alone (Annual Survey of Industries,
2025).
Over the period 1981–2023, aggregate employment elasticity remained

low at a virtually unchanged level, close to 0.3. This stability, however,
masks significant sectoral divergences: elasticity remained stable in
the services sector throughout the period (0.5), but it declined shar -
ply in the manufacturing sector (from 0.4 during 1981–1990 to just
0.1 during 2012–2023 2): at the same growth rate, India now creates
far fewer industrial jobs per unit of growth than it did forty years ago
(Table 2). In the formal manufacturing sector alone, employment elas-

ticity reached 0.7 over the past ten years, driven by very robust job
creation. However, this positive trend has not been sufficient to offset
the decline in informal sector employment, which accounts for 73.4%
of total manufacturing employment but only 38% of the sector’s value
added. Labour productivity in the formal manufacturing sector is thus
estimated to be 5.4 times higher than in the informal sector.
In capital-intensive sectors, employment elasticity ranged from 0.5 to

0.6 (including electrical equipment, transportation, chemicals and me-
tals), whereas in labour-intensive sectors — specifically wood, textiles
and apparel, and manufacturing food — employment elasticities re -
mained particularly low, or in certain cases, negative.

In other words, growth in value added in these sectors with high labour 
absorption potential is primarily driven by productivity gains rather 
than job creation. Furthermore, even among companies operating in 
the formal sector, employment elasticity remains low at an average of 
0.4. Consequently, the traditional mechanism facilitating labour mobi-
lity from the agricultural sector to the labour-intensive manufacturing 
sector has been very limited over the past forty years.
The only manufacturing subsectors that combine strong growth, high 
employment elasticity, and high productivity — thereby offering real 
potential for creating high-quality jobs — are electronics and infor -
mation technology, electrical equipment, the automotive industry, the 
chemical industry, and, to a lesser extent, the pharmaceutical industry. 
In these subsectors, the formal employment elasticity ranges from 0.8 
to 1.2 (Chart 2). However, in 2025, these jobs accounted for just 1.4% 
of total employment and 5.9% of GDP . The potential therefore remains 
negligible at the macroeconomic level.
Several structural constraints limit job creation in 
India
- The high rigidity of the labour market: it consists mainly of small, in-
formal businesses — those with fewer than 50 employees account for 
65% of the productive sector — largely due to the stringency of labour 
legislation (for example, companies with more than 300 employees 
cannot carry out mass layoffs without authorisation).
- Numerous obstacles impede the growth of industrial and job-crea -
ting businesses: an unfavourable business environment (particularly 
difficulties in accessing land acquisition to build factories); high costs 
of accessing capital (particularly for small and medium-sized enter -
prises); insufficient export competitiveness in labour-intensive sectors 
(textiles, clothing), which encounter disadvantages, including less ad -
vantageous tariff conditions3 than competing Asian economies.
- The mismatch between the skills of the available workforce and the 
needs of businesses. This mismatch constrains wage growth and the 
development of household purchasing power, obstructs industrial ad -
vancement, and, consequently, the creation of high-quality jobs.
INADEQUATE JOB QUALITY DUE TO A LACK OF  
APPROPRIATE SKILLS
A predominantly informal and low-paid labour  
market
According to the ILO, 87.2% of total employment in India is informal, 
with 73.4% concentrated in the manufacturing sector alone, predomi -
nantly engaged in low-value-added activities that require few skills. 
Furthermore, the Periodic Labour Force Survey (PLFS) 2023/24 indi -
cates that 58.4% of Indian workers are self-employed, 19.8% are day 
labourers, and only 21.7% are regular employees — the highest-paying 
status, with a wage gap of more than 60% compared to the other two 
categories. According to the ILO, 20% of the population captures 79% of 
aggregate labour income. The agricultural sector, which still employs 
41.5% of the labour force while contributing less than 15% to GDP , is

3 Historically, tariff rates imposed on Indian products exported to Europe have been higher than those applied to certain other Asian countries (Bangladesh, Vietnam, Pakistan).
4 According to the World Bank and the NCAER, educational and training programmes are too theoretical and fail to develop cognitive and interpersonal skills. Vocational education 
and training are not designed to meet the needs of high-demand sectors. Collaborative relationships between educational institutions and employers — particularly regarding 
internships and on-the-job training — remain inadequately developed.
3.7 times less productive than the services sector, with a median hourly 
wage that is 40 to 50% lower.
A widespread skills shortage
This concentration of earnings reflects the low skill levels of the jobs 
held: according to the PLFS, 88.2% of workers hold jobs requiring little 
or no skill. The proportion of skilled jobs is just 11.2% and has declined 
by 4.6 pp since 2018. The underlying challenge is not a shortage of 
graduates but rather a misalignment between what the labour market 
needs and the educational preparation provided to graduates 4, which 
results in their gradual downward mobility into positions far below 
their formal qualifications.
Unemployment, the downward mobility of graduates, and a shortage 
of technical skills
This disparity between skills and business needs directly contributes to 
unemployment among graduates, which reached 13.5% in 2025 accor-
ding to the ILO, compared with 1.3% for individuals without a degree. 
Within the 25- to 34-year-old demographic, the unemployment rate 
stands at 15.2% for graduates (19.2% for those with a master’s de -
gree), compared with an average of 6.1% across this age group. Only 
8.3% of graduates secure positions that genuinely correspond to their 
educational attainment; 53% of bachelor’s degree holders and 36% of 
master’s degree holders are overqualified for their positions.
In low-skilled jobs, the lack of technical training represents a signifi -
cant barrier to youth employment: according to the World Bank’s En -
terprise Survey (2022), 17.8% of traditional service companies and 14% 
of “intermediate” manufacturing companies cite this as a major obsta-
cle. In India’s manufacturing sector, 32.9% of positions are reportedly 
held by underqualified workers, according to the ILO, compared with 
28.8% in Indonesia and just 16.5% in Vietnam.
A direct impediment to industrial advancement
A survey conducted by the Ministry of Skill Development & Entrepre -
neurship across seven key sectors — automotive, textiles, agriculture, 
livestock, IT, commerce and green energy — reveals that the pace of

### Page 5

5
EcoInsight 20 / 26 October 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
workforce training development significantly lags behind industry re -
quirements. This forces companies to train a considerable number of
young graduates themselves before they are ready for the workforce.
According to the Telecom Talent in the 5G Era report by the Telecom
Sector Skill Council, only 40% of IT and telecommunications graduates
are actually immediately employable.

This disparity in skills particularly constrains the transition towards
higher-value-added manufacturing activities within the sectors iden -
tified above (automotive, electronics and chemicals). Their growth
trajectory depends on technological advancement — electric vehicles,
semiconductors, specialty chemicals — and therefore on intermediate
to advanced technical skills that are currently lacking. Should trai -
ning provision fail to align with industry demand, employment elasti -

city and growth in these sectors will stagnate or decline, undermining
the only source of high-quality job creation currently available — and
consequently, the Indian economy’s ability to expand its middle class.
These structural limitations affecting national industrial development
diminish its appeal to foreign investors, despite its size. The stock of
foreign investment in India stood at USD 560 billion in Q2 2026, repre-
senting just 15% of GDP (compared with 20% in China, more than 52%

in Vietnam, and around 67% of GDP in Thailand and Malaysia).
ARTIFICIAL INTELLIGENCE: A NEW RISK TO JOB CREATION
Artificial intelligence (AI) may also limit the expansion of high-paid
employment opportunities in the service sector. Its deployment poses
a significant threat to employment in India, particularly in the hi -
ghest-paying service sectors — the same sectors that possessed si -
gnificant potential for expanding India’s middle-class consumer base.

Agriculture (41.5% of the labour force), construction (12%), and trade/
transportation (18%) remain largely immune to the direct replacement
of workers by AI, which reduces the overall macroeconomic scale of
the coming shock. The service sectors offering the most lucrative em -
ployment — IT, finance and insurance — account for just 1.3% and 1.2%
of total employment, yet these remain the primary targets of automa-
tion. In IT services companies, the adoption of AI is expected to reduce

the need for entry-level roles (quality assurance, software testing and
support roles) but increase demand for positions centred on platform
development and management oversight. Current sector-specific evi -
dence indicates a notable slowdown in job creation within IT services
but no widespread contraction. A study by the Indian Council for Re -
search on International Economic Relations (ICRIER) on the IT sector
confirms a decline in job creation for routine and entry-level roles.

While new professions are emerging such as data centre operators, AI
DevOps engineers who serve as intermediaries between software deve-
lopment and systems operations teams, and ethical AI specialists, the
volume of employment opportunities generated remains substantially
lower than the number of jobs at risk. Furthermore, the skill require -
ments for these newly created positions exceed those possessed by the
existing cohort of graduates.
REFORMS ARE A STEP IN THE RIGHT DIRECTION BUT DO

NOT GO FAR ENOUGH
Since 2019, the Modi government has implemented a series of mea -
sures to develop industry, attract foreign direct investment, and gain
greater integration in global trade (after decades of protectionist po -
licies).
Corporate subsidy programmes (Production-Linked Incentives) have
helped support the development of those sectors with the greatest
potential for job creation (electrical, electronics, chemicals, and auto -
motive). Similarly, labour market reform (which came into force in late

2025) may alleviate impediments to business expansion and the crea-
tion of formal jobs. However, its actual impact will depend on its adop-
tion by the states, which retain considerable discretionary autonomy
over labour law, and on its effective implementation on the ground.
The free trade agreement signed between India and the European
Union in late January 2026 (which is expected to take effect in 2027 at
the earliest) should mitigate the tariff disadvantage experienced by la-

bour-intensive industries: the EU has pledged to remove tariffs, which
currently range from 4% to 12% on textiles and clothing.
However, these measures alone will be inadequate for creating
high-quality jobs if the education system fails to meet the needs of
Indian businesses. It is therefore imperative that India continues to
improve its business environment and, even more importantly, reform
its education system to promote job creation in high-value-added and

well-paying sectors. Without concrete measures, the “demographic
window” — which is expected to remain favourable for another 15 to
25 years — could close before India establishes a robust industrial
foundation and a substantial middle class.

*****
While India’s economic growth has demonstrated considerable dy -
namism, this growth has not translated into the development of the
middle class. The real medium-term concern involves the emergence
of a “neo-precarious” middle class — substantial in terms of num -
bers but lacking the foundations necessary for advancing towards a
high-income economy (households with stable employment, the ability

to save, and social protection). The creation of formal, high-quality jobs
is currently inadequate to sustain industrial expansion and its progres-
sion towards higher-value segments. The cultivation of certain niche
activities, while promising, remains very limited and is far from benefi-
ting the entire population. Finally, the development of AI poses more of
a risk than a genuine opportunity for the country’s development. India
lacks both the infrastructure and the educational system necessary to

deploy AI in a way that would yield tangible benefits for its economy
and labour market.
Article completed on 19 October 2026

## Document 473: US MARKETS CALL_ S&P 500 Valuation Dives As Earnings Soar

Source PDF: all report pdf/US MARKETS CALL_ S&P 500 Valuation Dives As Earnings Soar.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-11-07
Pages: 12
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US MARKETS CALL: S&P 500 Valuation Dives As Earnings Soar
Date:    5 hours ago
US MARKETS CALL: S&P 500 Valuation Dives As Earnings Soar

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US MARKETS CALL: S&P 500
Valuation Dives As Earnings
Soar
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 7 Nov 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Baptiste Azais / Unsplash
The stock market dived last week. Investors priced in political backlash
against data centers after Democrats swept Congress. We anticipated
buying opportunities after the midterm elections, and Thursday’s selloff
delivered some. This remains an earnings-led bull market. Valuations are
falling while earnings are soaring. We still view the AI backlash as another

### Page 3

speed bump, not a roadblock. We are sticking with our 7,900 year-end
target for the S&P 500.
I. Performance
The Nasdaq entered correction territory on Thursday and closed the week
below its 200-day moving average (chart). It has done this before recently.
It rebounded sharply after its Spring 2025 "Liberation Day" plunge and
again after the Iran war started earlier this year, quickly resuming its
uptrend both times.
The equal-weight S&P 500 is holding up remarkably well. It remains near

its recent record high and above both its 50-day and 200-day moving
averages (chart). The damage has been concentrated in the AI trade, not
the broader market.

### Page 4

The Magnificent-7 are underperforming again. The MAGS ETF is up only
2.4% ytd, versus 7.8% for the S&P 500 (chart). The Impressive-493 also
took a hit this week. The XMAGS ETF includes Micron and the other
semiconductor stocks hit hardest in the selloff. It is still up 10.0% ytd.
Dow Theory remains bullish. The Dow Jones Industrial Average is holding
up well near its highs (chart). The Transports have lagged. They should
improve if oil prices continue to fall.

II. Earnings
Earnings season picked up this week. The blended Q3 S&P 500 EPS
growth estimate (i.e., actual results for companies that have reported,
estimates for those that haven’t yet) rose to 25.9% y/y from 23.8% a week
earlier, helped by AMD’s solid results. Q4’s estimate edged down to 29.7%

### Page 5

(chart). As we noted previously, Q2’s 50.4% y/y EPS growth rate was
boosted by mark-to-market gains at Alphabet and Amazon. Excluding
those, growth was closer to 25%, which is still very strong.
Forward earnings rose to a record $418.25. It is converging on the 2027
estimate of $426.28, which continues to climb (chart). Analysts’ 2028
consensus S&P 500 EPS estimate rose to $495.14; it’s been closing in on
$500—which is our target for 2030!  We are bullish, but not as bullish as

the analysts. The drop in the forward P/E this year suggests that investors
also believe analysts are too bullish on earnings.
Forward earnings for the S&P 500, S&P 400, and S&P 600 all rose to
record highs again last week (chart). Earnings strength remains broad.

### Page 6

S&P 500 forward revenues per share is up 15.9% y/y, while forward
earnings per share is up 39.0% y/y (chart).
III. Valuation
The S&P 500 forward P/E fell to 17.6 last week, below the 2025 Liberation
Day low (chart). P/E plunges typically accompany corrections and
recessions. The economy shows no sign of a recession anytime soon.
Investors instead fear irrational exuberance in earnings forecasts.

### Page 7

Since the bull market began on October 12, 2022, the S&P 500 has risen
106.2%. Forward earnings is up 75.5%, while the forward P/E is up 22.5%
(chart). The P/E and E both drove the rally until the forward P/E peaked in
October last year. Since then, E has done all the work. Investors are
finding it harder to predict the return on all the AI capital spending. They
won’t pay an excessive multiple given that uncertainty.
The S&P 500 Semiconductors industry accounts for much of the P/E drop.

The industry index's forward P/E has plunged to 13.1, well below the S&P
500’s (chart). Semis are now cheaper than they were during the 2022
bear market.

### Page 8

IV. Fixed Income
The futures market expects about two rate hikes over the next 12 months.
That explains why the 2-year Treasury yield remains well above the 3.88%
federal funds rate, even after easing to 4.46% following the midterms
(chart).
The 10-year Treasury yield continues to fall. It dropped to 4.84% and is
testing its uptrend line from last year’s low (chart). We’ll see if it bounces.
Either way, it is back in the 4.00%-5.00% “old normal” range where we feel

comfortable.

### Page 9

The 10-year TIPS yield fell to 2.62%. The yield spread between the 10-
year Treasury and TIPS, a widely used proxy for expected inflation, eased
to 2.22% (chart). That proxy tends to move with oil prices. The recent drop
in crude showed up here.
V. Commodities
The price of Brent crude oil fell to $98.32 a barrel in recent days (chart).
No news of the re-escalation of the war in the Middle East has emerged
since the midterms. Hopes for a US-Iran deal are reflected in the oil

market. We still see a risk of re-escalation.

### Page 10

Nearby gasoline futures dropped to $2.78 a gallon last week (chart). If
sustained, that should start to show up as lower inflation in a couple of
months. Renewed fighting in the Middle East would reverse that.
Steel prices show no sign of a slowdown in data center construction. US
Midwest hot-rolled coil rose to $1,357 a ton, the highest since 2022
(chart).

### Page 11

Agricultural prices have eased. The S&P GSCI Agriculture Spot index fell
to 376.77 last week (chart). Fears of a food price spike should subside.

Source PDF: all report pdf/【趨勢主題】歐洲市場問答：高利環境下的壓力與機會.pdf

歐洲市場問答：高利環境下的壓力與機會
1
Thematic Report
重點提示
中東地緣衝突自2026年2月底爆發以來仍陷僵局，能源價格維
持高檔的時間較預期更長，令歐元區通膨壓力與升息預期升溫。
另一方面，歐洲政府擴大財政支出，公債供給增加，加上歐洲
央行持續縮表，推升期限溢價，令歐洲公債殖利率大幅走升，
德國十年期公債殖利率9/28升破3.6%，創2009年6月以來新高。
本報告透過問答，分析歐洲公債殖利率走高的原因、主權債務
與財政風險、政府因應措施、高利率對企業的影響，以及高利
率環境下的歐洲股債展望。
◼ 本波歐洲公債殖利率走高的驅動因素：能源價格上漲推升通
膨、市場重估歐央貨幣政策路徑，同時，財政支出令歐洲公

債供給增加與歐央持續縮表使私人部門需要吸收更多公債供
給，推升期限溢價，財政疑慮使部分歐洲國家利差承受額外
壓力，加上全球利率上升的外溢影響，共同推升殖利率走高。
◼ 歐洲政府調整發債結構，央行備有穩市工具：歐洲政府縮短
發債期限以控制融資成本，預估此趨勢將延續至2027年；
萬一主權債市場出現無序波動，歐洲央行有OMT與TPI政策
工具，但不會輕易動用。
◼ 整體歐洲債務風險尚可控但中長期壓力持續累積：目前利率
水準尚未對整體債務可持續性構成普遍威脅，但財政脆弱性
較高的國家壓力升高，法國尤其需要關注。財政與政治變數
使法國資產風險溢價維持高位，目前股債定價已反映相當程
度的負面風險。
◼ 德國公債殖利率短期將維持高檔：歐洲央行12月仍有再升息
可能，待能源價格與通膨壓力緩和、升息預期降溫後，利率
才有回落空間，但難降至3%以下。
◼ 歐洲企業資產負債表健康、獲利具韌性，緩衝高利率衝擊。
◼ 高利率限制歐股估值擴張，歐股維持中立，聚焦獲利成長與
政策支撐的產業：銀行受惠於目前利率環境與信貸需求，工

業、電氣化/能源安全及AI受惠戰略自主與資本支出提升。
Sep. 29, 2026
林亞瑱
歐洲總經政策與市場分析

德國公債殖利率曲線上移，短率升幅大於長率
中東衝突發生以來德國公債殖利率曲線(中東地緣衝突前 vs. 目前)

東地緣衝突爆發以來起算 ，德國十年債利率累積彈升了近 100
個基點，30年期德債利率亦逼近4%(至9/28止)。
Q1：什麼因素推動今年歐洲公債殖利率走高？
(1)能源價格上漲推升通膨、市場重估歐央貨幣政策路徑，為驅
動本波利率上揚的主因：
2月底中東衝突爆發以來 ，德國10年期公債殖利率與油氣價格
走勢高度相關。此外，德國公債各期限殖利率全面上移 ，其中
短率升幅高於長率 ，反映本波利率上揚主 要受到能源價格上升
所帶動的通膨風險，以及市場對ECB政策路徑重新定價的影響。
此外，美國殖利率上升透過全球資本市場產生外溢效果 ，帶動
全球無風險利率中樞上移 ，亦對歐洲公債殖利率形成上行壓力 。

歐洲政府發債規模增與ECB縮表同步推升債券供給
■淨債券發行 ■ECB縮表 ◆對私人部門的淨供給
(2)歐洲公債供給增加、央行持續縮表，推升期限溢價：
德國擴大財政支出，歐洲政府融資需求及公債供給增加；同時 ，
ECB持續進行資產負債表正常化 ，逐步減少公債持有量 ，使新
增債券供給需由私人投資人吸收 ，推升投資人要求的期限溢價 。
除此之外，政府債務持有者結構轉變 ，個人與海外投資人等對
價格較為敏感的投資人占比增加，使債市更容易受到市場情緒
與金融條件變化的影響。
(3)財政疑慮使部分歐洲國家利差承受額外壓力：
歐洲部分國家面臨較高的政府債務壓力，加上政治局勢限制財
政調整空間，市場對債務可持續性的疑慮難消 。尤其在年度預
算協商期間，財政脆弱性較高的國家如法國 、英國，主權債務

風險重新定價容易反映在長端殖利率及與德債的利差擴大。

涉及特定投資標的之建議。
Q2：面對公債殖利率上升，歐洲政府如何因應？
面對公債殖利率上升 ，歐洲政府調整發債結構 、增加短年期債
券發行，以控制高利率環境下的融資成本 ，預期此趨勢將延續
至2027年；惟縮短債務期限雖可降低當期融資成本，也將提高
中期再融資風險。
Q3：萬一歐洲主權債市場出現無序波動，歐洲有哪些
政策工具可以運用？
歐洲央行主要政策工具有二 ，分別為直接貨幣交易 (OMT)與傳
導保護工具(TPI)。
(1)直接貨幣交易(OMT)：2012年9月推出，源自歐債危機，可
在次級市場購買符合條件國家的1至3年期政府公債，且購買
規模事前不設上限 ，惟須搭配歐洲穩定機制 (ESM)的金融援
助計畫，並具嚴格的附帶條件，使用門檻較高，由主權國家

主動申請。
(2)傳導保護工具 (TPI)：2022年7月推出 ，可在次級市場購買
符合條件國家的 1至10年期政府部門債券 ，目的在於因應與
該國基本面不相稱的無序市場波動 ，避免金融市場分裂影響
貨幣政策傳導。啟動時將評估該國是否符合歐盟財政規範 、
債務是否具備可持續性等條件 ，由歐央評估後決定是否啟動。
不過，OMT與TPI均屬市場失序時的最後手段 ，並非用於壓低
正常的主權債殖利率或消除國家間的基本面利差 ，啟動有嚴格
條件，歐央不會輕易動用，目前此兩項工具均未實際動用過。
高利率下，歐洲政府調整發債、央行備有穩市工具

Q4：歐洲政府債務的可持續性是否有立即風險？
主要歐洲國家除德國與西班牙外 ，IMF預估2026年政府債務佔
GDP比重均超過 100%。同時，近年地緣衝突頻率升高 ，戰略
自主與強化地區安全已成為歐洲核心目標 。為了讓成員國有擴
大國防支出的空間 ，歐盟允許成員國啟動 國家豁免條款，得以
在增加國防支出的同時 ，暫時不受歐盟財政規則約束 ，截至目
前已有18個歐盟會員國申請。國防支出提升，若未搭配增加財
政收入或其他支出調整，將使政府債務維持在較高路徑。
所幸，從利率與經濟成長率差距來看 ，目前利率水準尚未對歐
洲整體債務可持續性構成 立即威脅，但部分高負債國家的債務
壓力仍須留意。過去十年低利率環境的效益逐步消退 ，利率與

經濟成長率的差距 (r－g)較過去不利 ，加上期限溢價回升 ，使
高負債國家的債務動態面臨風險。
檢視歐元區四大經濟體 ，德國、西班牙政府債務的利率低於經
濟成長率，債務動態相對穩健；義大利政府債務 佔GDP比重最
高，所幸在政府控制支出下，自2024年以來維持初級財政盈餘，
有助控制債務比率進一步擴大。法國利率雖略低於經濟成長率 ，
但政府債務已接近 GDP的120%，在財政赤字偏高 、利息支出
持續增加且財政整頓進展有限 的情況下，未來兩年債務佔GDP
比率預期將持續上升，財政脆弱性相對較高。
整體歐洲債務風險可控但中長期壓力正在累積

Q5：近期法德利差顯著擴大，驅動因素與對法國股債
市的影響？
財政與政治不確定性升溫 ，推升法國主權風險溢價 ：法國步入
財政與政治不確定性升溫時期 ，不利的財政前景 ，加上4Q26
預算協商與2027年將舉行總統大選，政治不確定性升溫推升十
年期法德利差9月下旬突破100個基點，9/28升至112個基點、
創2012年6月以來新高。法國總統大選將於2027年4~5月舉行，
4/18舉行第一輪投票 ，若無候選人過半 ，5/2將舉行第二輪投
票。由於法國政治版圖高度分裂 、且極右派支持度領先 ，相較
於過往選舉周期，2027年法國選舉的不確定更高，預估將令市
場在2027年總統大選前維持謹慎情緒。
民調反映政治版圖分裂 ，增加財政政策的不確定性： 民調顯示，
極右派國民聯盟候選人瑪琳·勒龐在總統大選首輪的支持度領先，
超過三成，極左派法國不屈黨候選人梅朗雄 、中間偏右候選人
愛德華·菲利普民調落在15~18%左右。法國大選高機率將會進
入第二輪投票，取決於進入第二輪的候選人 ，目前民調顯示以

中間偏右候選人菲利普最有希望擊敗勒龐 ，若為極左派梅朗雄
進入第二輪，則勒龐勝選機率較高。

極端政治勢力候選人在本次大選勝選的可能性提高 ，為本已脆
弱的法國財政增添另一層不確定性 。與以往政治週期不同 ，法
國進入2027年大選週期時，財政迴旋餘地相當有限，加上法國

股債市影響：財政與政治變數將令法國資產風險溢價維持高位
(1)債市： 巴克萊資本 (2026/8)預估三種情境： (a)正向情境：
中東衝突告一段落 、秋季預算協商順利 、中間派候選人民調上
升，十年期法德利差有機會收斂至60~65個基點；(b)不利情境：
秋季預算協商引發市場雜音 、中東衝突進一步推高升息預期 ，
總統大選民調維持較高不確定性 ，法 德 利差 預 估 將 擴 大 至

95~100個基點；(c)極端情境：沒有中間派候選人進入第二輪
總統大選，法德利差可能突破100個基點。
9/28十年期法德利差已升至 112個基點，顯示市場已反映相當
程度的政治風險，再大幅惡化的空間應有限 。儘管如此，不確
定性短期難消，預估法德利差仍將維持在(b)情境高檔盤整。
(2)股市：法國股市估值相較歐股處於折價，且投資人對法國股
市的配置已明顯降低 ，有助緩和股市大幅修正的風險 。然而，
短線亦缺乏驅動股市行情的催化劑 ，法國經濟成長預測持續下
修、經濟動能落後歐元區 ，加上政治不確定性將影響投資人配
置意願，預估法國股市在2027年4月總統大選前波動仍大 ，金
融與內需導向型企業估值預估將承壓。

Q6：德債利率後續走勢？
貨幣政策正常化與結構因素 ，德債利率中樞上移： 低實質利率
環境已改變，根據歐洲央行執委Schnabel(2025/2)分析，過去
壓低實質利率的部分結構性因素正 在逆轉。一方面，地緣政治
碎片化、氣候變遷及勞動力短缺 ，使中長期通膨上行風險增加；
另一方面，全球由「儲蓄過剩」逐步轉向「債券供給過剩 」，
政府財政赤字擴大與央行資產負債表正常化 ，使安全資產供給
增加、稀缺性下降 ，進而推升實質利率 。疫情後實質利率上升
究竟是暫時現象 ，抑或代表新的長期均衡目前雖難預測 ，但從
全球儲蓄與投資平衡 的變化，地緣政治與氣候變遷等結構挑戰 ，
實質利率上升似乎是較有可能出現的情況。
利率短期維持高檔 ，待通膨與升息預期緩和才有回落空間： 能
源價格衝擊與通膨傳導將是牽動後續利率的主要因素 。歐央
2026年已升息二碼，存款機制利率上調至2.5%的中性區間上緣。

中東衝突持續下 ，歐央上調2027~2028年通膨預期 ，預計中期
內通膨率和核心通膨率將高於2%政策目標。目前天然氣期貨價
格已偏離歐央基準情境 、往不利情境靠攏 ，成品油價格因裂解
利差跳升而居高不下 ，因此，除非中東局勢迅速平息 ，否則預
估12月將再升一碼至 2.75%。9/28利率期貨反映 市場預期2027
年9月前將再升息超過3碼。不過，高能源價格若 延續較久，將
抑制經濟成長 ，殖利率上升也將發揮緊縮效果 、削弱通膨傳導 ，
我們預期歐央持續升息的必要性低於目前市場定價 。後續利率
上行空間將取決於能源價格與通膨是否進一步超出預期 ，待通
膨與升息預期緩和 ，利率將有回落空間 。惟考量結構性因素推
升，德國10年期公債殖利率預期將維持在3%以上；若要回落至
3%以下，可能需要看到歐央再次調降政策利率 ，預估4Q27才
有機會降息。

Q7：高利率對歐洲企業的影響？
首先，歐洲企業資產負債表整體維持健康 ，歐洲企業近年持續
去槓桿，淨債務/EBITDA比率維持相對低檔 ，整體財務槓桿不
高。而且，企業債務期限普遍較長 ，平均到期期限約五年 ，高
利率對企業利息負擔的傳導較為緩慢 ，因此，即使公債殖利率

上升，企業債務成本的重新定價將逐步反映 ，降低企業對利率
上升的敏感度。
另一方面 ，企業信貸需求仍具韌性 ，8月歐元區非金融企業貸
款年增4.2%，顯示截至目前企業融資活動並未因利率走高而明
顯降溫。此外，儘管歐洲公債殖利率大幅走高 ，歐洲投資級債
及非投資級債信用利差並未明顯擴大 ，顯示企業信用基本面 並
未出現明顯惡化。
整體而言，企業資產負債表維持健康 ，融資需求仍具韌性 ，信
用風險未明顯升高，高利率目前對企業的直接衝擊有限。
資料來源：Bloomberg，2005年以來至2026/9/23，中國信託商業銀行彙整，2026/9。
歐洲企業債信用利差維持低檔

Q8：歐股展望？
2026年以來至9/28止，歐洲道瓊50指數上漲9%，過去一個月
出現回檔 ，能源價格再度上漲 、TTF天然氣期貨價格飆升至三
年多高點，央行立場偏鷹、公債殖利率大幅走高 ，加上歐洲政
治雜音增加，均對盤勢形成壓力 。然而，歐股自高點以來波段
回檔不到5%、幅度可控，企業獲利維持韌性扮演重要緩衝。
STOXX歐洲600企業2026年上半年每股盈餘較去年同期成長

14%，創近3年來最快增速 。雖然獲利成長主要由商品類股貢
獻，但商品以外類股上半年每股盈餘年增率亦上修至 7%。高
盛證券(2026/8)預估，2026年下半年歐洲企業獲利年增率將進
一步加速至19%，全年獲利預估成長15%。
短線歐股盤勢將受到油價與利率牽動 ，過去一個月德國十年期
公債殖利率跳升 36個基點 ，壓抑歐股評價 ，預估本益比降至
14.7倍(9/28)。目前美伊仍透過斡旋進行接觸 ，惟尚未看到達
成和解的明確路徑 ，在能源價格維持高檔 、通膨上行風險仍存
之下，歐央可能進一步升息，此外，2027年歐洲多國進入選舉
周期，政治不確定性將干擾市場情緒 ，令本益比擴張空間受限。
2027年企業獲利增速預估將由 2026年的高雙位數放緩至中個
位數，但仍維持穩定成長 ，將為股市提供基本面支撐 ，惟股市
動能預期將較為溫和。
Q9：高利率環境下，歐股布局聚焦哪些方向？
利率維持高檔、指數評價擴張空間有限下 ，歐股展望維持中立 ，
相對看好具獲利成長或政策支撐的產業 。銀行股受惠於目前利

率環境及企業信貸需求回升；工業 /國防、電氣化/能源安全及
AI等概念股則受惠於政策支持及相關投資需求增加。

銀行股受惠目前利率環境與信貸需求回升
銀行股獲利穩健，且在殖利率上升環境表現相對較佳 。歐洲央
行政策利率維持在 2%–3%區間，對銀行業有利 ；企業信貸需
求回升，有助銀行淨利息收入維持成長 ，摩根大通(2026/9)預
估，2026、2027年歐洲銀行NII將分別成長9%及6%。此外，
歐洲銀行CET1資本比率維持高檔 ，反映銀行具備充足的資本
緩衝。在上述因素支撐下 ，預估歐洲銀行2025~ 2028年提存

前營業利益年均複合成長8.8%，銀行獲利具韌性。
歐洲強化戰略自主，相關產業受益資本支出提升
歐洲強化戰略自主，工業/國防、電氣化/能源安全及AI等領域
受政策支持，將帶動相關投資需求 。隨著德國財政刺激逐步落
地，國防、基礎建設、能源及AI投資推進，有助支撐相關產業
的獲利動能。相較之下，能源價格維持高檔更久 ，對消費支出
構成風險，令消費股面臨逆風 ，需待地緣衝突緩解 。從獲利動
能來看，戰略自主受惠產業獲利動能持續優於消費類股 ，顯示
具政策與投資需求支撐的戰略產業獲利展望相對明確。
殖利率上升環境，金融股相對受惠
歐洲產業相對大盤表現與德國10年期公債殖利率週變動的相關性

## Document 626: 債券市場總評：非投等高息有撐

Report source: Fubon
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 7
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

台北富邦銀行 
Oct 5，2026 | 3:00 PM (GMT+8) 
債券市場總評： 非投等高息有撐
投資研究部 
 Research 
Financial Market 
▎美國經濟具韌性，支撐年底升息預期 
9 月下旬以來，美債殖利率快速上行，主要因經濟數
據仍具韌性，加上中東情勢反覆推升油價與通膨疑
慮，牽動市場持續評估後續升息空間。雖然 8 月 PCE
與核心 PCE 低於預期，但先前公布的 9 月製造業與服
務業 PMI 大幅優於預期，第 2 季 GDP 終值上修，顯
示經濟動能仍強。但最新 9 月非農意外疲弱，新增就
業僅 2.9 萬人、失業率升至 4.2%，降低短期升息急迫
性，10 月升息機率降溫，但 12 月升息預期仍存，市
場轉向觀望 9 月 CPI 公布，10 年期美債殖利率短暫回
落後仍處高檔。 
圖 1 就業數據降溫，惟美債殖利率仍處高檔

資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10/5 
▎殖利率攀升至高位，債市同步下滑 
近日部分 Fed 官員表示，後續利率決策仍需觀察更多
數據，略為緩和市場對進一步升息的定價，帶動 10 年
期美債殖利率自高點小幅回落至 5.25%。不過，殖利
率與油價仍處高檔，限制債市修復空間。9 月下旬以
來，主要債券 YTD 報酬同步下滑，美國公債與投資級
債跌幅較深，非投等債則因存續期較短、風險偏好尚
穩，跌幅相對較輕。

圖 2 利率與油價高檔，主要債券報酬下滑

資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10/5 
◼ 近期美國數據仍具韌性 ， 中東局勢推升油價與通膨疑慮 ，PCE 雖低於預期 ，但 PMI
與 GDP 仍強，非農轉弱緩和升息預期，美債殖利率仍處高檔，債市承壓。美非投
等債受高息收與中短存續期緩衝 ， 跌幅較輕 ， 殖利率升至8.3%， 處近10 年高位。 
◼ 美非投等債信用結構較過去改善，較高評級的 BB 級占比已逾半，違約率約 2%，
位於近 25 年低檔。美非投等企業 2Q 營收與 EBITDA 分別年增 10.4%、14.4%，
增速加快並創近 4 年較強表現 ， 獲利增速快於營收 ， 也帶動槓桿與償息指標改善。 
◼ 殖利率高檔震盪下，債券報酬較難仰賴價格貢獻，息收成為更重要的收益來源。
美非投等債因票息較高、存續期較短，可提供一定報酬緩衝。後續可依風險承受
度，關注科技、金屬與礦業、能源等基本面改善較明顯產業，並偏向 BB、短中天
期及現金流較穩定發行公司。

美國投等債報酬率 -2.74% 
美國公債報酬率 -3.13%

9 月非農就業新增 2.9 萬人 
9 月失業率 4.2% 
8 月 PCE 年增率 3.4% 
8 月核心 PCE 年增率 3%

### Page 2

誠信親切、專業創新 週報 
▎高息收提供緩衝，支撐非投等債韌性 
近期公債殖利率快速上行 ， 推升企業債殖利率至近年相
對高位，其中美國非投資等級債到期殖利率已來到約
8.3%，顯著高於過去 10 年平均，殖利率水準已具備相
當吸引力。 
圖 3 美非投等債殖利率攀升至 8.3%，10 年相對高檔

資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10/1

觀察近 6 個月各類債券報酬來源 ， 今年美國公債殖利率
攀升，使債券報酬較難仰賴價格貢獻，息收重新成為影
響總報酬的關鍵來源。其中，美國非投資等級債因具備
較高殖利率與中短存續期，且息收水準相對較高，較能
緩衝利率上行對價格的影響， 因此相較公債與投等債，
回落幅度相對有限。

圖 4 利息收益貢獻為債券報酬主要來源

資料來源：JPM，2026/9/30

▎美經濟相對穩健 ， 非投等BB 比重提升 
從已開發國家 GDP 年增率來看，美國、歐元區與日本
未來幾季仍維持正成長， 其中美國成長表現相對穩健 ，
預估仍維持約 2%左右增速 ， 顯示高利率與油價波動下 ，
經濟仍具一定韌性。

圖 5 美國經濟動能仍相對歐元區、日本較具韌性

資料來源：JPM，2026/9/26

信用結構方面， 美國非投等債市場債信結構已較金融海
嘯前改善，BB 評級占比由 2007 年 12 月的 32%升至
2026 年 5 月的 54%，目前占整體非投等債比重過半，
CCC 與未評級占比則由 15%降至 7%。 由於BB 是非投
等債中評級最高的一層，信用風險低於 B 與 CCC，BB
級占比提高代表目前美國非投等債市場中 ，信用體質較
接近投資等級的發行公司占比明顯提升。 
圖 6 美國非投等債各信用評級占比與違約率

資料來源：JPM，2026/8/31

%

### Page 3

3

誠信親切、專業創新 週報 
違約率方面，目前美國非投等債整體違約率約 2%，落
在歷史區間相對低位 ， 低評級債違約壓力也未見明顯擴
散。在美國經濟仍具韌性、非投等債 BB 比重提升且違
約率受控的背景下 ， 美國非投等債的高殖利率仍具基本
面支撐。

▎美非投等企業，2Q 營收獲利顯著改善 
檢視美國非投等債企業第 2 季基本面，營收與 EBITDA
延續正成長動能，且2Q26 成長幅度進一步加速。美國
非投等債企業營收季增 9.2%、 年增10.4%，EBITDA 季
增 19.2%、 年增14.4%， 營收與EBITDA 年增率雙雙創
下 4 年來最高，顯示企業營運表現仍具韌性。

圖 7  美非投等企業營收年增 10.4%，4 年來最快增速

資料來源：JPM，2026/9/15

從產業表現來看，今年商品、能源與企業投資需求仍延
續，金屬與礦業、能源、科技、零售與服務業等產業對
整體營收與 EBITDA 成長形成貢獻，使 2Q 基本面改善
具備一定廣度。EBITDA 增速高於營收增速，代表企業
獲利擴張速度快於收入成長，反映成本控管、營運效率
或價格轉嫁能力仍在。 
圖 8 EBITDA 年增顯著提升至 14%，4 年來較強增速

資料來源：JPM，2026/9/15 
▎美非投等槓桿回落，償息能力改善 
2Q 美國非投等債企業信用指標出現改善，槓桿率降至 
4.51 倍 ， 主要反映EBITDA 成長速度快於債務增加 ， 帶
動槓桿指標修復。

圖 9 美非投等企業槓桿率回落至 4.51 倍

資料來源：JPM，2026/9/15

償息能力方面，儘管利息費用仍在增加，但 EBITDA 成
長仍帶動利息保障倍數回升至 4.12 倍，2 年較高水準 ，
顯示企業獲利對利息支出的覆蓋能力有所改善 ， 高利率
環境下，美非投等企業的償息能力仍相對有撐。整體來
看，2Q26 美國非投等債企業營收、獲利與信用指標同
步改善，基本面韌性仍在。

圖 10 美非投等企業利息保障倍數升至 2 年高位

資料來源：JPM，2026/9/15

### Page 4

4

誠信親切、專業創新 週報 
▎非投等具高息收優勢 ， 關注BB 短天期 
近期公債殖利率仍在高檔波動 ， 美非投等債短線表現受
到壓抑。不過，經過利率重新定價後，美非投等債殖利
率已回到相對具吸引力的水準 ， 較高息收與相對較短存
續期，仍可在利率波動環境下提供一定報酬緩衝。 
綜合前述，美國經濟相對歐日仍較穩健，企業營收、獲
利與信用指標同步改善 ， 為美非投等債後續表現提供基
本面基礎。產業面可留意科技、基礎工業相關的金屬與
礦業、能源等基本面改善較明顯的方向。整體而言，後
續可偏向 BB、短中天期及現金流較穩定的發行公司，
參與較高息收帶來的收益機會。

3Q26 反映漲價效應，營運顯著優於預期 
台光電 3Q26 營收 608.9 億元(季增 28.8%/年增 142.1%)，毛利率 37.7%，
季增 3.8ppts/年增 7.6ppts，優於預期的 35.1%，主因 1)已反應原料價格上
漲；2) 基礎設施約季增 32%為成長最強勁的產品線、行動裝置約季增 16%、
汽車 /工業 /其他約季增 25%，同時 M7 以上比重進一步提升至 65-70% 
(2Q26 為 60-65%)。營業利益 186.9 億元，季增 46.7%/年增 274.5%，優於
預期 21%；獲利方面，業外包含補助款 1.07 億元、利息收入、匯兌等貢獻，
整體 3Q26 稅後獲利 145 億元，季增 46.9%/年增 265.7%，EPS 40.47 元。 
4Q26 將反映下一輪漲價，同時高階 CCL 將更為吃緊 
展望 4Q26/1Q27，本中心認為隨高階 CCL 吃緊、原物料價格進一步上揚，
估計台光電將以此營運基礎更向上，同時預計 N 公司、ASIC 等新一代產品
開始量產，預計台光電產能將持續維持滿載，並預計崑山廠擴產進度往前，
預計高階 CCL 10 萬張提前供應，1Q27 也將有崑山跟中山新產能開出，本
中心分別上調 4Q26/1Q27 營收 23%/15% 至 717.4/769.4 億元 ( 季增
17.8%/7.3%)，毛利率 39.1%/39.8%，營業利益 231.9 億元/253.9 億元，季
增 24.1%/9.5%，EPS 上修 34%/23%至 50.1/53.68 元，季增 23.8%/9%。 
台光電新產能持續開出，整體成長趨勢明確，維持買進 
展望 2026/27 年，產能仍滿載、訂單能見度更長、傳輸規格升級明確，預期
產品組合將持續提升，估計 M7 比重將提升至 75-80%，本中心上修 EPS 
18%/18%至 133.02/268.89 元。台光電整體成長趨勢明確，同時也看到公司
積極準備後續擴產所需的原料，預期後續高階 CCL 效益將更為顯現，儘管
短期觀音廠因土建及驗收而延遲，不過台光 1Q27-2Q27 仍有新產能持續開
出，得以因應客戶強勁需求。本中心維持 27 倍本益比並以 2027 年 EPS 進
行評價，推得目標價 7,260 元，維持「買進」評等。 
元大觀點 
⧫  
3Q26 營收 608.9 億元，毛利率 37.7%，優於預期的 35.1%，主因漲價
效應及 M7 以上產品比重提升至 65–70%。 
⧫  
供需持續吃緊，4Q26 新一輪漲價，加上 N 公司及 ASIC 新產品量產、
崑山新產能提前開出，上修 4Q26/1Q27 EPS 至 50.10/53.68 元。 
⧫  
需求持續強勁，預估 M7 以上產品比重將升至 75–80%；上修 2026/27
年 EPS 至 133.02/268.89 元，目標價 7,260 元，維持買進評等。

現高階 CCL 產能供不應求，台光電規劃至 2030 年的產能 
目前台光電為具備高階 CCL 產能的廠商，目前已觀察到高階 CCL 需求持續強勁，同時客戶目前給
予訂單能見度拉長，現台光電規劃 2030 年產能將達 1525 萬張，叫 2026 年的 585 萬張成長 160%：
以台光電最新的各年度產能規劃如下，1) 原定 12M26 將開出的觀音廠產能因土建及驗收延遲，因
此遞延至 2H27 方能開出，且原訂後續的 60 萬張則遞延至 2029 年，2026 年總產能將為 585 萬張；
2) 2027 年規劃為 900 萬張產能：1Q27 將加入崑山 50 萬張新產能、中山 60 萬張、2Q27 檳城 15
萬張、此外中山廠 60 萬張、觀音廠 60 萬張、黃石 30 萬張、觀音廠 30 萬張將於 2027 年底完成擴
充；3) 2028 年將加入崑山 55 萬張、中山 90 萬張產能，總產能共計 1045 萬張；同時隨觀音、中
山、檳城進一步擴產，2029/2030 年產能將達 1195 萬張/1525 萬張，體產能擴充趨勢明確，且目
前台光電已為全球最大的 CCL 廠商，具備最全面的客戶群及高階 CCL 產能，台光電龍頭地位穩固。

高階 CCL 趨勢不變，預期 M7 以上比重持續增加 
展望 2026/2027 年，整體 CCL 廠商產能仍趨於滿載，客戶洽談訂單能見度更長，以目前產能方不
足以滿足客戶需求，主因 AI Server、Switch、General Server，皆已見需求浮現，傳輸規格升級越
加明確，AI Server 方面也將隨新一代陸續往 M8+/M9 規格升級，預期產品組合將持續提升，同時
美系網通廠也進一步指出 1.6T 將為 2027 年最大成長動能並優於 800G 出或，整體 Switch 需求將
隨 Scale up 應用而明顯提速，估計台光電 M7 比重將陸續提升至 75-80%，綜合上述，本中心就此
次財報調整 2026/2027 年 財 務 預 估 ， 預 估 2026/2027 年 營 收 2129.6/3665.1 億 元 ， 年 增
125.9%/72.1%，上修幅度 10%/11%，毛利率 36%/40%，營業利益 617.4/1249.8 億元，年增
223.1%/102.4%，稅後獲利 476.6/963.5 億元，年增 225.4%/102.1%，EPS 133.02/268.89 元，上
修幅度 18%/18%。

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第4 頁，共 26頁  
產品結構分析  
台光電 AI 伺服器與高速網通帶動基礎設施營收占比提升 
台光電主要供應銅箔基板（CCL）與黏合片（Prepreg），為 PCB 製造所需之基礎材料，產品涵蓋
不同損耗等級，應用由智慧型手機延伸至 AI 伺服器、雲端資料中心、高速網通及車用電子。受惠
AI 伺服器及高速網通需求成長，基礎設施業務 (包含 AI 伺服器、高速網通、衛星 )占營收比重由
3Q25 的 65–70%提升至 3Q26 的 75–80%，同期行動裝置及車用／工業／其他業務預估占比分別
為 10–15%及 5–10%。3Q26 基礎設施營收季增 32%、年增 176%，台光電成長主要來自產品漲價
與高階材料出貨組合改善，亦估計帶動 M7 以上材料營收占比由 1Q26 的 55–60%提升至 3Q26 的
65–70%。

圖 3：基礎建設業務項目(F)

圖 4：基礎設施營收占比持續提升

資料來源：元大投顧預估 資料來源：公司資料、元大投顧預估

高速與低 CTE 材料並進，產品布局延伸至載板及 Physical AI 
隨 AI ASIC 及 1.6T 交換器升級，M8 產品 EM-892K2(目前需求以 1.6T Switch 為主)於 2H26 放量，
2027 年需求可望進一步增溫；M9 產品 EM-896 系列已於 3Q26 小量量產，相關產品多採 HDI 及至
少四次壓合設計且除追求更低 Df 外，也保留較低 CTE 及較高剛性，預期 2027 年將顯著貢獻；
M10 產品 EM-898K4 採 Q-Glass 方案，Df 降至 0.0004，預計 2027 年有望小量出貨。材料發展除
追求低損耗外，更重視低 CTE、翹曲控制及 HDI 加工良率，並透過 Hybrid 混壓結合高速與載板材
料，兼顧成本、電性及可靠度。載板材料方面，EM-S572T 採 T-Glass，具超低 CTE 約 0.8ppm/°C
及 40GPa 高模數，目前開始延伸至 CSP 等載板應用，4Q26 已開始量產。此外，台光電透過 A 系
列產品服務電動車、工業與人形機器人等 Physical AI 應用。原料方面，銅箔及玻纖布供應有限，
公司優先配置予 M8 以上產品。同時台光電也已規劃未來至 2030 年的產能將達 1525 萬張，後續
新增產能以 M8 以上為主。本中心正向看待高階 CCL 升級、載板材料滲透及產能擴張帶動 2027 年
營運成長。

主要供應鏈；隨平台演進至 VR200，Switch Tray 材料往 M8+（M8.5）升級，PCB 層數由約 24 層

工複雜度提高。AWS Trainium 3 依氣冷與水冷方案分別採約 26 層及 40 層 PCB，使用 M8 級高速
材料；Meta 相關 ASIC 亦採用 M8 級材料，台光電均已切入上述供應鏈，在 Google 及 AWS 相關
平台預計為主要供應商，台光電持續受惠 TPU v9/Trainium 4 的 CCL 規格持續升級，加上 AMD、
Meta 等客戶或相關專案提供額外成長動能。

### Page 7

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第7 頁，共 26頁  
同業比較分析  
台廠皆陸續推出高階材料，規格升級仍為 CCL 產業趨勢 
台光電 M8 主力 EM-892K／K2 於 10GHz 下的 Df 分別為 0.0017／0.0013，M9 的 EM-896K2／K3
則進一步降至 0.0009／0.0008；就同業高階料號而言，台燿 TU-953R／Q 為 0.0008／0.0006、聯
茂 IT-999GSE2／GSE3 為 0.0009／0.0007，南亞 NPG-999K／K2／Q 則為 0.00083／0.0007／
0.00045，各家均已建立 Df 低於 0.001 的產品選項。進入 M10 後，台光電 EM-898K4 與聯茂 IT-
Q10GSE4 的 Df 均約 0.0004，台燿 HC 系統 TU-963F／Q 分別為 0.00036／0.0004，南亞 NPG-
999Q 則為 0.00045，電性數據已落在相近區間。

台光以 Q 布方案為主、台燿並行 HC 與 PTFE 方案、南亞以自有材料拓展高階產
品 
在下一代材料路線上，台燿 TU-963Q 採 HC 碳氫樹脂搭配石英布、TU-963F 為 HC 無布方案，另
有 PTFE 無布產品 TU-973F，並提出 TU-973 作為 Core、TU-963 作為 Prepreg 的混壓設計，在電
性之外兼顧多層板壓合及加工需求；不過，無布材料的機械支撐與 PTFE 多層板加工性，仍須依實
際疊構與製程驗證。台光電 M10 主要以碳氫樹脂材料配合 Q Glass 為主，聯茂 IT-Q10GSE4 則採
HC 樹脂、Q 布及 HVLP4／5 銅箔，南亞則搭配自行開發的 M9 樹脂，並整合 Low-Dk 玻纖布與
HVLP 銅箔，持續推進高階 CCL 產品。

圖 8：台光電、台燿、聯茂、南亞高階 CCL 產品布局

台光電高階高速材料占比領先同業，優先受惠規格升級

General Server 及 Telecom；HDI／N-LL 19%；Others 15% 主要對應 Power 與厚銅材料。聯茂則
仍以 M4 以下產品為主，比重約 70%，短期較受惠中低階材料漲價，後續升級動能包括 Intel Oak 
Stream、AMD Venice 等一般伺服器平台導入 PCIe Gen6 ，帶動材料由 M6 轉向 M7，以及美系
GPU 加速器導入 M9。南亞 M 系列占 CCL 營收比重則由 2Q26 不到 20%，現已提高至約 30%，惟
其中 M4 仍占逾半，廣泛應用於消費性電子與一般伺服器，其餘規格又以 M6 最多，顯示現階段產
品結構仍偏向中低階材料。本中心認為，台光電目前高階高速材料營收比重較高，且已廣泛參與
GPU、ASIC 與高速交換器等應用，為 AI 基礎設施材料規格升級的優先受惠廠商。

及高速交換器需求，進一步鞏固高階 CCL 供應優勢。

財務分析  
台光電營收獲利持續成長，各項財務指標穩健 
台光電 2021–2025 年流動比率與速動比率分別維持於約 146–178%及 117–141%，2025 年分別為
160.0%及 122.4%，顯示短期流動性具一定緩衝。負債比率則由 2021 年的 45.9%升至 2024 年的
53.9%，2025 年小幅降至 53.3%，反映擴產及營運規模擴大下的資金需求。2022 年及 2024 年負
債增加與發行可轉換公司債有關。 
獲利能力方面，ROE 由 2021 年的 30.1%降至 2022／2023 年的 24.3%／22.5%，2022 年手機與
筆電相關出貨減少，雖由基礎設施產品成長支撐，全年營收仍僅微增 0.45%，營運成長受到消費性
電子需求疲弱限制，合併稅後淨利減少 7.7%且可轉債轉換增加導致權益增加較多。後續回升至
2024／2025 年的 30.9%／34.3%。主因 2024–2025 年公司則受惠有利產品組合，帶動 ROE 連續
回升，股東資金報酬率隨營運改善而提升。 
經營能力方面，應收帳款收現天數由 2021 年的 110.0 天延長至 2023 年的 128.2 天，後續回落至
2025 年的 120.1 天；應付帳款付現天數則由 2021 年的 89.7 天延長至 2025 年的 111.7 天。存貨週
轉天數於 2021–2024 年維持約 59–63 天，2025 年升至 72.3 天，存貨及應收帳款金額增加主要與
銷售規模持續擴大有關。儘管 2025 年庫存天數上升，收現天數下降及付款天數延長抵銷了大部分
增加的資金占用，使現金轉換週期由 2024 年的 79.8 天小幅增至 2025 年的 80.6 天，整體營運資金
週轉尚未明顯惡化。 
利息保障倍數由 2021 年的 91.6 倍降至 2022／2023 年的 35.2 倍／24.3 倍， 2024–2025 年隨產品
組合改善及獲利成長，帶動利息保障倍數回升至 27.4 倍及 40.7 倍。2021-2025 低點仍逾 24 倍，
顯示獲利對利息費用具有相當覆蓋能力，擴產期間的償債能力仍有營運獲利支撐。

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第12 頁，共 26頁  
產業概況  
AI、ASIC、通用型強勢出擊，2026 年美系 CSP 資本支出估年增 91%  
在 ChatGPT 等 AI 應用陸續推向市場，各大業者紛紛開始研發自有超級大模型，包括 Gemini 
(Google)、Claude (Anthropic) 、Llama (Meta) 等，各大品牌開始大舉投入大型語言模型(LLM)市場，
且每一個產品的智慧皆持續提高，部分消費者也陸續從 ChatGPT 陣營轉移至 Gemini 陣營，反映
出消費者開始逐漸提升對 AI 的黏著度。根據 Grand View Research 調查，2024 年全球 AI 市場營
收約 2,792 億美元，預估 2030 年市場規模超過 3 兆美元，擁有超過 30% 的年複合增長率。 
面對這爆發性的長期成長趨勢，CSP 業者(Amazon、Microsoft、Google、Meta)在法說會上一致強
調，AI 投資已轉變為一項橫跨 2026 年的結構性戰略，CSP 大廠不僅持續擴大對 GB300 等次世代
AI 伺服器的採購，更將重點放在資料中心基礎設施的長期戰略規劃，以應對 AI 運算對電力及散熱
系統強勁成長的需求，確保未來數年內擁有足夠的能源與容量來支持持續迭代的大模型部署。本中
心認為，2026 年美國四大 CSP 資本支出將年增 91%，且將有一半的成長來自於資料中心建置，代
表了資料中心的不足及未來伺服器需求。明年不僅僅是 NVIDIA GB300 的天下，AWS、Google、
Meta ASIC 的崛起也將是一大關鍵，其中又以 Google TPU 需求最為強勁，已經有兩個客戶預計將
採購 TPU。 
另外，受惠於 AI 訓練與推論需求爆發的連帶效應，本中心預估 2026 年通用型伺服器出貨量將年增
23%，其中以存儲型伺服器需求最為強勁。隨著生成式 AI 應用普及和使用者數量暴增，CSP 等業
者必須不斷擴充容量，且不僅限於高階 GPU 伺服器，龐大的 AI 模型訓練和推論在使用狀態中將消
耗大量的數據(DATA)，使得存儲型伺服器的需求大增。

### Page 13

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第13 頁，共 26頁  
ASIC 進入高速成長期，GPU 需求仍維持擴張 
從 AI 晶片預期出貨來看，GPU 與 ASIC 需求均持續成長，但 2026 年後 ASIC 成長動能明顯加速。
Nvidia GPU 出貨量預估由 2025 年 565.7 萬顆增至 2026 年 650 萬顆、2027 年 850 萬顆，年增
17%及 34%；AMD 亦由 2025 年 90 萬顆增至 2027 年 180 萬顆，顯示通用 GPU 算力需求仍維持
成長。 
相較之下，ASIC 受惠 CSP 加速自研晶片及導入規模擴大，總出貨量預估由 2025 年 380 萬顆大幅
增至 2026 年 725 萬顆、2027 年 1,360 萬顆，年增率達 91%及 88%，成長速度明顯高於 GPU。其
中 Google 仍為最大 ASIC 使用者， 2027 年用量預估達 800 萬顆，AWS 亦增至 320 萬顆，
Microsoft 及 Meta 同步擴大部署。我們認為，在資料中心電力逐漸成為算力擴張瓶頸下， CSP 將
更重視每瓦運算效能及整體運算成本， ASIC 針對特定運算工作的效率優勢有望推升滲透率，惟
GPU 憑藉通用性及軟體生態系優勢，仍將維持重要地位。

圖 16：GPU/ASIC 出貨量預估

資料來源：元大投顧

AI 伺服器規格及需求顯著提升，未來可見 PCB 往多層板升級 
隨著 AI 晶片(如 NVIDIA GPU 及 CSP ASIC) 不斷迭代，伺服器和網路設備的 PCB/CCL 規格呈現
爆 炸 性 增 長 ， 層 數 和 尺 寸 都 大 幅 攀 升 。 如NVIDIA 方 面 ， 隨 著 GPU 從 A100/H100 演 進 至 
GB200/GB300，再到下一代 Rubin 系列， PCB 層數持續增加， GB200/GB300 Compute Tray 
(Bianca Board) 採用 HDI 結構，層數約為 22 層(5+12+5 設計)，Rubin 系列預計層數將升級至 24-
26 層，材料也需從 M8 級升級至更高規格；CSP 方面，AWS 將往 26 層架構、Google 往 44 層、
Meta 也將往 38-40 層架構提升。往多層式架構升級，主因為應對 AI 晶片帶來的兩大核心挑戰：電
源完整性(PI)和訊號完整性(SI)，更高層數的 PCB 提供足夠的訊號走線空間、完整的電源與地平面，
以及穩定的回流路徑，以達成超高速訊號的阻抗控制、提升供電完整性與整體訊號品質。  
2026 年 ASIC 晶片量大增，材料升級為明顯趨勢 
2024 年開始 ASIC 需求逐步提升，主要來自 Google 及 AWS，帶動整體 AI 市場持續升溫。進入
2025 年，AWS Trainium 2 、Google TPU v6 的 ASIC 伺服器產品需求大幅提升，其中 AWS ASIC
晶片產量預計將達到 160 萬顆，下半年則會有新一代 ASIC 晶片推出，包括 Trainium 2.5 及 Meta 
MTIA 2 等。展望 2026 年，隨著 Meta MTIA 3 、AWS Trainium 3 及 Google TPU V7 等產品逐步
上市，隨高速需求帶動材料端持續升規，預期 CCL 將陸續由 M7 升級至 M8/M9，玻纖布升級至
LDK、LDK2、Q 布；銅箔也將升級至 HVLP3/4。

### Page 14

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第14 頁，共 26頁
高階 CCL 對銅箔、玻纖布規格要求持續提高
CCL 的核心組成包括，銅箔 (占成本約 40–50%)、玻纖布(25–40%)以及樹脂(20–30%)，三者經過
高溫高壓的壓合後成為 PCB 的關鍵介電材料。其中銅箔負責導電，玻纖布提供結構補強，而樹脂
則扮演兩者的黏結與介電性能調控角色。隨著伺服器傳輸速度大幅提升，高頻高速訊號對材料的穩
定性、損耗控制與結構強度要求同步加嚴，使得上游銅箔與玻纖布的等級成為影響  CCL 廠競爭力
的核心關鍵。
銅箔方面，銅箔在 CCL 中最核心的角色是導電與訊號傳輸。高速 PCB 的線路愈來愈細小，訊號傳
輸速率愈高，對銅箔的三大要求也同步升級：1)更低粗糙度(Ra 需降至 0.5 μm 以下)，以降低界面
散射造成的插入損耗； 2)更高平整度，確保訊號完整性、減少阻抗； 3)更高可靠度，承受大面積
GPU/ASIC 封裝下的熱循環壓力。因此，新一代 AI 板材大量採用 HVLP3、HVLP4、甚至 HVLP5。

玻纖布方面，其最大要求在於提升機械強度與維持板材穩定度。 GPU/ASIC 封裝尺寸變大，使
PCB 在熱負載下更容易翹曲，因此玻纖布的 CTE(熱膨脹係數)也變得比以往更重要。傳統 E-Glass
難以應付高速與熱衝擊，因此材料開始升級至 Low Dk1、Low Dk2，再到 Q-Glass。這些高階布料
除介電損耗更低、散佈更均勻外，其低 CTE 特性可讓 PCB 更耐熱、更抗翹曲、更耐層間應力，是
支撐高階 CCL 的關鍵材料。
綜合而言，AI 應用推動的高階 CCL，不再只是樹脂與銅箔性能的疊加，而是上游原料(銅箔、玻纖
布)與下游 CCL 設計共同協作的結果。因此掌握高階銅箔與布料的供應能力，已成為  CCL 廠能否
在 AI 時代維持領先與擴大市占的關鍵。
圖 17：愈高階 CCL，Dk/Df 要求愈高
CCL Dk (介電常數) Df (介電損耗) 銅箔
M6 3.8 <0.006 HVLP2
M7 3.5 <0.005 HVLP3
M8 3.0 <0.0015 HVLP3/4

M9 <3.0 <0.0007 HVLP4/5
資料來源：元大投顧整理
Low Dk/Low Df 電子級玻纖布製程要求精細，並已見石英布規格
在高速運算與 AI 伺服器的要求下，玻纖布製造難度大幅提升。整體難點來自三大核心環節：抽紗、
編織與表面處理。首先在抽紗端，愈高階布 (如 Low Dk2 、Q-Glass)纖維直徑愈細、均質度要求愈
高，任何溫度或孔徑偏移都會影響  Dk/Df，使高階抽紗線的速度更慢、良率更低。其次在編織階段，
細纖維容易斷紗，張力需極度穩定，布面不得出現波紋、跳針或皺折，否則會造成  CCL 平整度下
降、訊號完整性受損，這使得高階布的織布速度必須明顯降低。第三是表面處理 (上膠)，需以矽烷
界面讓玻纖能與樹脂良好結合，而 Q 布則因其窯爐溫度較玻纖布高(需達 1,700–2,000 度)，鑽孔、
雷射加工、除膠等工藝製程步驟皆需重新設計，不過其熱膨脹係數低、 Dk/Df 明顯具備優勢，因此
未來 CCL 往 M9 以上規格提升時，已將 Q 布解決方案考量進來，如台光電 EM-896K3、台燿 TU-

953Q 等皆以 Q 布取代玻纖布。
相關供應廠商仍以日系 Nittobo、Asahi 為主，相關台廠包含富喬 (1815 TT ，未評等)、台玻(1802
TT，未評等)、南亞(1303 TT，未評等)、建榮(5340 TT，未評等)。

### Page 16

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第16 頁，共 26頁  
2026 年 PCIe 從 5.0 升級至 6.0，帶動 Connector/Cable 用量及 ASP 增加 
因 Scale-up 僅暫時將規範定義出來，在 2027-2028 年才會真正落實，不過 2026 年開始加速滲透
至 PCIe 6.0 ，又 NVIDIA 曾在 GTC 表示，為了跟上兆參數模型的規模，必須升級整個堆疊，如
GPU、CPU、網路、記憶體，這也是為什麼打造下一代 Vera CPU ，配備先進記憶體與 PCIe/CXL 
架構，以消除未來 AI 工廠中的所有瓶頸。 
因此在更高的 CPU 效 率 及 記 憶 體 擴 展 下 ， 加 速PCIe (Peripheral Component Interconnect 
Express) 、 CXL (Compute Express Link) 於 Server 間的升級， PCIe 通訊對象包含
CPU→GPU/SSD/NIC 等裝置之間的資料交換，如前述，為提升整體效能， PCIe 規格往 5.0、6.0
升級 (圖 3)，6.0 以上版本調頻技術由 NRZ 改使用 PAM4 ( 由以往的 1bit 升級至 2 bit ，代表頻寬翻
倍) 使傳輸資料翻倍。 
隨著伺服器傳輸協定由 PCIe 5.0 演進至 PCIe 6.0 ，訊號傳輸速度翻倍，這也對 PCB 的核心基材銅
箔基板(CCL)提出了極其嚴苛的物理挑戰。PCIe 6.0 首次引入了 PAM4 調變技術，雖然在相同頻率
下頻寬翻倍，但其訊號電壓準位更窄，這意味著對訊號完整性的容錯率大幅下降。為了抑制隨頻率
升高而劇增的介電損耗，板材必須從 PCIe 5.0 所使用的 Very Low Loss (M6) 等級，全面升級至 
Ultra Low Loss (M7) 或甚至 Super Ultra Low Loss (M8)等級。

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第17 頁，共 26頁  
獲利調整與股票評價  
維持台光電買進評等，目標價 7,260 元 
展望 2026/2027 年，整體 CCL 廠商產能仍趨於滿載，客戶洽談訂單能見度更長，以目前產能 不足
以滿足客戶需求，主因 AI Server、Switch、General Server，皆已見需求浮現，傳輸規格升級越加
明確，AI Server 方面也將隨新一代陸續往 M8+/M9 規格升級，預期產品組合將持續提升，同時美
系網通廠也進一步指出 1.6T 將為 2027 年最大成長動能並優於 800G 出或，整體 Switch 需求將隨
Scale up 應用而明顯提速，估計台光電 M7 比重將陸續提升至 75-80%，綜合上述，本中心就此次
財 報 調 整 2026/2027 年 財 務 預 估 ， 預 估 2026/2027 年 營 收 2129.6/3665.1 億 元 ， 年 增
125.9%/72.1%，上修幅度 10%/14%，毛利率 36%/41%，營業利益 617.4/1249.8 億元，年增
223.1%/102.4%，稅後獲利 476.6/963.5 億元，年增 225.4%/102.1%，EPS 133.02/268.89 元，上
修幅度 18%/18%。 
評價方面，本中心認為台光電整體成長趨勢明確，同時也看到公司積極準備後續擴產所需的原料，
預期後續高階 CCL 效益將更為顯現，儘管短期觀音廠因土建及驗收而延遲，不過台光 1Q27-2Q27
仍有新產能持續開出，得以因應客戶強勁需求。本中心維持 27 倍本益比並以 2027 年 EPS 進行評
價，推得目標價 7,260 元，維持「買進」投資評等。

公司簡介  
台光電子材料股份有限公司成立於 1992 年，為專業 CCL ( 銅箔基板)製造商，據研調機構 Prismark 統計，2023 年台光電
CCL 總產值世界第一，市占率約 33%，顯示台光電耕耘高階 CCL 市場多年之成果。目前台光電擁有桃園廠、新竹廠、昆
山廠、中山廠及黃石廠共 5 座工廠， 2025 年底 CCL 產能約 555 萬張/月，預計 2026 年底總產能最終達 615 萬張/月，同時
也預計於 2027 年底達 930 萬張/月。

### Page 23

請參閱末頁之免責宣言。 台灣：PCB 2026 年 10 月 29 日 Elite Material 第23 頁，共 26頁

ESG 分析  
分別藉由「在 ESG 議題上的曝險」和「個別公司在 ESG 議題上的執行力」兩個面向來衡量公司於 ESG 上的表現。 
► ESG 總分： 台光電整體的 ESG 風險評級屬於低度風險，於 Sustainalytics 資料庫所覆蓋的公司中排名亦排行於領先的
位置，在科技硬體行業的公司中亦領先同業。 
► 在 ESG 議題上的曝險： 該子行業的公司通常面臨最高風險，涉及重大 ESG 問題，即碳自有營運、商業道德和資源使
用。 
► 個別公司在 ESG 議題上的執行力：台光電的資訊揭露力度強，反映了其對投資者和公眾的高度責任感。該公司採取了
一些措施來管理與重大 ESG 問題相關的風險，但缺乏關鍵領域的政策和方案。儘管如此，該公司尚未捲入任何與 ESG
相關的重大爭議
